应对埃博拉病毒第 50 周年:绘制埃博拉病毒物种之间的诊断、疫苗 和治疗差距

On the 50th year responding to Ebola: mapping diagnostic, vaccine, and therapeutic gaps across ebolavirus species
作者： Jiahui Si, Jiachun Cai, Junhui Zhou, Lili Wang, George F. Gao, Jun Liu
摘要中文翻译今年是 埃博拉病毒 识别 50 周年,但 2026 年本迪布焦埃博拉病毒病 暴发 暴露了埃博拉防范策略的重要局限性,这些策略仍然主要集中于扎伊尔埃博拉病毒。尽管诊断、疫苗 和治疗方面的重大进展是在 2014-2016 年西非埃博拉疫情之后取得的重大进展,但大多数获得许可的对策都是针对扎伊尔埃博拉病毒制定的,并且可能对其他埃博拉病毒物种(包括本迪布焦埃博拉病毒)提供有限的保护。在此,我们研究了本迪布焦埃博拉病毒的流行病学意义,并回顾了当前和新兴的诊断方法、疫苗、抗体疗法和 抗病毒药物 策略,重点关注其物种覆盖范围和局限性。我们进一步讨论了诊断盲点和有限的物种包容性对策如何在当前的 暴发 期间造成挑战。未来的埃博拉防范工作应采用更广泛的框架,涵盖具有流行潜力的多种埃博拉病毒物种。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
该综述检查了引起 埃博拉病毒病 的人类致病性正埃博拉病毒(埃博拉病毒、苏丹病毒、本迪布焦病毒、塔伊森林病毒、扎伊尔埃博拉病毒),重点关注埃博拉病毒物种之间的诊断、疫苗 和治疗差距。核心问题涉及目前的对策是否充分,这些对策主要以扎伊尔埃博拉病毒为中心,以解决由本迪布焦埃博拉病毒等其他埃博拉病毒物种引起的 暴发 问题。
证据基础与方法
该综述是一种叙述性综合,评估当前和新兴的诊断方法、疫苗、抗体疗法和 抗病毒药物 策略,强调其物种覆盖范围和局限性。它讨论了 2026 年本迪布焦埃博拉病毒 暴发 期间的诊断盲点和物种包容性对策挑战。
共识与结论
自 2014 年至 2016 年西非埃博拉疫情以来,诊断、疫苗 和治疗方面取得了重大进展,但大多数获得许可的对策是针对扎伊尔埃博拉病毒制定的,并且可能对其他埃博拉病毒物种(包括本迪布焦埃博拉病毒)提供有限的保护。
争议与缺口
诊断盲点和有限的物种包容性对策在 2026 年本迪布焦埃博拉病毒 暴发 期间造成了挑战,暴露了主要针对扎伊尔埃博拉病毒的埃博拉防备策略的重要局限性。
科研与实践启示
未来的埃博拉防范工作应采用更广泛的框架,涵盖具有流行潜力的多种埃博拉病毒物种,以解决诊断和对策方面的差距。

On the 50th year responding to Ebola: mapping diagnostic, vaccine, and therapeutic gaps across ebolavirus species

Authors: Jiahui Si, Jiachun Cai, Junhui Zhou, Lili Wang, George F. Gao, Jun Liu
Original abstract (English)
This year marks the 50th anniversary of the Ebola virus identification, but the 2026 outbreak of Bundibugyo ebolavirus disease has exposed important limitations in Ebola preparedness strategies that remain largely focused on Zaire ebolavirus. Although major advances in diagnostics, vaccines, and therapeutics have followed the 2014-2016 West African Ebola epidemic, most licensed countermeasures were developed against Zaire ebolavirus and may provide limited protection against other ebolavirus species, including Bundibugyo ebolavirus. Herein, we examine the epidemiological significance of Bundibugyo ebolavirus and review current and emerging diagnostics, vaccines, antibody therapies, and antiviral strategies, with emphasis on their species coverage and limitations. We further discuss how diagnostic blind spots and limited species-inclusive countermeasures contributed to challenges during the current outbreak. Future Ebola preparedness should adopt a broader framework encompassing multiple ebolavirus species with epidemic potential.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The review examines human-pathogenic orthoebolaviruses (Ebola virus, Sudan virus, Bundibugyo virus, Taï Forest virus, Zaire ebolavirus) causing Ebola virus disease, focusing on diagnostic, vaccine, and therapeutic gaps across ebolavirus species. The central question addresses the adequacy of current countermeasures, which are largely Zaire ebolavirus–centric, in addressing outbreaks caused by other ebolavirus species such as Bundibugyo ebolavirus.
Evidence base and method
The review is a narrative synthesis that evaluates current and emerging diagnostics, vaccines, antibody therapies, and antiviral strategies, emphasizing their species coverage and limitations. It discusses diagnostic blind spots and species-inclusive countermeasure challenges during the 2026 Bundibugyo ebolavirus outbreak.
Consensus and conclusions
Major advances in diagnostics, vaccines, and therapeutics have occurred since the 2014–2016 West African Ebola epidemic, but most licensed countermeasures were developed against Zaire ebolavirus and may provide limited protection against other ebolavirus species, including Bundibugyo ebolavirus.
Controversies and gaps
Diagnostic blind spots and limited species-inclusive countermeasures contributed to challenges during the 2026 Bundibugyo ebolavirus outbreak, exposing important limitations in Ebola preparedness strategies that remain largely focused on Zaire ebolavirus.
Research and practice implications
Future Ebola preparedness should adopt a broader framework encompassing multiple ebolavirus species with epidemic potential to address diagnostic and countermeasure gaps.

现代的 埃博拉病毒病:发病机制、治疗进展、疫苗 策略和全球健康准备

Ebola virus disease in the modern era: pathogenesis, therapeutic advances, vaccine strategies, and global health preparedness
作者： Dola Sundeep, Kovuri Umadevi, Jaweria Masood, Nayanika Tummala
摘要中文翻译埃博拉病毒病 (EVD) 仍然是全球公共卫生系统面临的最严重、最复杂的病毒性出血热之一。自 1976 年首次出现以来,主要由扎伊尔埃博拉病毒引起的反复出现的 暴发 已经证明了丝状病毒具有非凡的流行潜力,特别是在医疗基础设施脆弱的地区。2013-2016 年前所未有的西非疫情从根本上改变了对 埃博拉病毒、传播、发病机制、临床进展和 暴发 管理的科学认识。随后在刚果民主共和国和乌干达进行的 暴发 进一步强调了 埃博拉病毒病 带来的持续风险,尽管诊断、治疗和 疫苗接种 策略取得了进展。埃博拉病毒 感染的特点是广泛的免疫失调、异常的炎症反应、内皮功能障碍、凝血障碍和进行性多器官损伤。最近的研究显着扩展了对病毒持久性、埃博拉后综合征、宿主 免疫应答 和病毒免疫逃避机制的理解。分子诊断、基因组监测、单克隆抗体疗法和重组疫苗平台的并行发展大大改善了生存结果和流行病控制措施。尽管印度没有经历过埃博拉疫情 暴发,但全球化和不断增加的国际流动性增加了输入性感染的可能性,因此需要强有力的防备框架。西非疫情爆发后,印度加强了机场 监测、实验室生物安全能力、综合疾病 监测 系统和紧急 暴发 响应方案。然而,在快速诊断、医疗基础设施、生物安全培训和公众意识方面仍然存在重大挑战。这篇综述批判性地审视了 埃博拉病毒 流行病学、分子生物学、发病机制、临床表现、诊断、治疗、疫苗 开发和公共卫生准备方面的当前进展,特别强调对印度和未来全球卫生安全的影响。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
埃博拉病毒病 (EVD) 仍然是全球公共卫生系统面临的最严重、最复杂的病毒性出血热之一。这篇综述批判性地审视了 埃博拉病毒 流行病学、分子生物学、发病机制、临床表现、诊断、治疗、疫苗 开发和公共卫生准备方面的当前进展,特别强调对印度和未来全球卫生安全的影响。
证据基础与方法
自 1976 年首次出现以来,主要由扎伊尔埃博拉病毒引起的反复出现的 暴发 已经证明了丝状病毒具有非凡的流行潜力,特别是在医疗基础设施脆弱的地区。2013-2016 年前所未有的西非疫情从根本上改变了对 埃博拉病毒、传播、发病机制、临床进展和 暴发 管理的科学认识。
共识与结论
埃博拉病毒 感染的特点是广泛的免疫失调、异常的炎症反应、内皮功能障碍、凝血障碍和进行性多器官损伤。最近的研究显着扩展了对病毒持久性、埃博拉后综合征、宿主 免疫应答 和病毒免疫逃避机制的理解。
争议与缺口
在快速诊断、医疗基础设施、生物安全培训和公众意识方面仍然存在巨大挑战。
科研与实践启示
分子诊断、基因组监测、单克隆抗体疗法和重组疫苗平台的并行发展大大改善了生存结果和流行病控制措施。尽管印度没有经历过埃博拉疫情 暴发,但全球化和不断增加的国际流动性增加了输入性感染的可能性,因此需要强有力的防备框架。西非疫情爆发后,印度加强了机场 监测、实验室生物安全能力、综合疾病 监测 系统和紧急 暴发 响应方案。

Ebola virus disease in the modern era: pathogenesis, therapeutic advances, vaccine strategies, and global health preparedness

Authors: Dola Sundeep, Kovuri Umadevi, Jaweria Masood, Nayanika Tummala
Original abstract (English)
Ebola virus disease (EVD) remains one of the most severe and complex viral hemorrhagic fevers confronting global public health systems. Since its first recognized emergence in 1976, recurrent outbreaks caused primarily by Zaire ebolavirus have demonstrated the extraordinary epidemic potential of filoviruses, particularly in regions with fragile healthcare infrastructure. The unprecedented West African epidemic of 2013-2016 fundamentally altered scientific understanding of Ebola virus transmission, pathogenesis, clinical progression, and outbreak management. Subsequent outbreaks in the Democratic Republic of Congo and Uganda have further emphasized the persistent risk posed by Ebola virus disease despite advances in diagnostics, therapeutics, and vaccination strategies. Ebola virus infection is characterized by extensive immune dysregulation, aberrant inflammatory responses, endothelial dysfunction, coagulopathy, and progressive multiorgan injury. Recent studies have significantly expanded understanding of viral persistence, post-Ebola syndrome, host immune responses, and mechanisms of viral immune evasion. Parallel developments in molecular diagnostics, genomic surveillance, monoclonal antibody therapy, and recombinant vaccine platforms have substantially improved survival outcomes and epidemic control measures. Although India has not experienced endemic Ebola outbreaks, globalization and increasing international mobility have heightened the possibility of imported infections, necessitating robust preparedness frameworks. Following the West African epidemic, India strengthened airport surveillance, laboratory biosafety capacity, integrated disease surveillance systems, and emergency outbreak-response protocols. However, substantial challenges remain regarding rapid diagnostics, healthcare infrastructure, biosafety training, and public awareness. This review critically examines current advances in Ebola virus epidemiology, molecular biology, pathogenesis, clinical manifestations, diagnostics, therapeutics, vaccine development, and public health preparedness, with particular emphasis on implications for India and future global health security.

Ebola virus disease (EVD) remains one of the most severe and complex viral hemorrhagic fevers confronting global public health systems. This review critically examines current advances in Ebola virus epidemiology, molecular biology, pathogenesis, clinical manifestations, diagnostics, therapeutics, vaccine development, and public health preparedness, with particular emphasis on implications for India and future global health security.

Controversies and gaps

乌干达和刚果民主共和国的跨境准备工作:对卫生安全能力、差距和优先事项的系统审查

Cross-Border Ebola Virus Disease Preparedness in Uganda and the Democratic Republic of the Congo: A Systematic Review of Health Security Capacities, Gaps, and Priorities
作者： Chinyere Nkemjika Anyanwu, Rachelle Soki Marasi, Regina Idu Ejemot-Nwadiaro, Bunu Umi Omar, Theoneste Hakizimana, Nwanganga Ihuoma Ubosi, Zeinab Adam Abakar Mohamed, Chima Paul Okechukwu Ugwu, Emeka Anyanwu, Umi Omar Bunu
摘要中文翻译背景:刚果民主共和国(DRC)经常发生的 埃博拉病毒病 (EVD) 暴发 继续对邻国(特别是乌干达)构成重大跨境卫生安全风险。尽管已经实施了许多准备举措来预防、检测和应对跨境埃博拉 传播,但有关其有效性、优势和持续差距的证据在同行评审和灰色文献来源中仍然支离破碎。本次审查综合了乌干达和刚果民主共和国跨境埃博拉防范工作的现有证据,并确定了加强区域卫生安全的关键能力、挑战和优先事项。方法 根据 PRISMA 2020 进行系统评价,并使用无荟萃分析综合 (SWiM) 框架进行报告。从 2000 年 1 月到 2026 年 5 月 18 日,我们对 PubMed、Scopus、Web of Science、非洲在线期刊 (AJOL) 和主要灰色文献来源进行了检索。结果综合了九个预定义的准备领域。结果 纳入 71 个证据来源,其中包括 32 项初步研究和 39 份灰色文献。证据主要集中在边境 监测 和入境点准备(24 个来源)、接触者追踪和快速反应(22 个来源)、跨境流动和输入风险(18 个来源)、感染预防和控制(17 个来源)以及区域治理和协调(17 个来源)。准备活动包括流动测绘、旅行者筛查、医护人员培训、实验室强化、风险沟通和跨境协调机制。该综合报告确定了 监测 系统、实验室准备、医疗设施准备和区域合作的大​​量准备能力。然而,一些主要的准备领​​域仍然存在重大差距。结论 过去二十年来,通过对 监测 系统、实验室网络、医护人员准备和区域协调的投资,乌干达和刚果民主共和国的跨境埃博拉准备工作大幅扩展。尽管如此,人道主义环境、备灾可持续性和新兴备灾领域仍然存在持续的脆弱性。加强综合、可持续和跨境卫生安全系统对于减轻未来的埃博拉威胁和加强整个东非的流行病防范至关重要。

范围与问题
本次审查重点关注乌干达和刚果民主共和国 (DRC) 的跨境 埃博拉病毒病 (EVD) 准备情况。核心问题是评估两国之间预防、检测和应对跨境埃博拉疫情的卫生安全能力、差距和优先事项。
证据基础与方法
根据 PRISMA 2020 进行系统评价,并使用无荟萃分析综合 (SWiM) 框架进行报告。证据来源是通过搜索 PubMed、Scopus、Web of Science、非洲在线期刊 (AJOL) 和 2000 年 1 月至 2026 年 5 月 18 日期间主要灰色文献来源确定的。研究结果是在九个预定义的准备领域进行综合的。

该综合报告确定了 监测 系统、实验室准备、医疗设施准备和区域合作的大​​量准备能力。过去二十年来,通过对乌干达和刚果民主共和国的投资,跨境埃博拉防备工作显着扩大。

科研与实践启示
加强综合、可持续和跨境卫生安全系统对于减轻未来的埃博拉威胁和加强整个东非的流行病防范至关重要。准备活动包括流动测绘、旅行者筛查、医护人员培训、实验室强化、风险沟通和跨境协调机制。

Cross-Border Ebola Virus Disease Preparedness in Uganda and the Democratic Republic of the Congo: A Systematic Review of Health Security Capacities, Gaps, and Priorities

Authors: Chinyere Nkemjika Anyanwu, Rachelle Soki Marasi, Regina Idu Ejemot-Nwadiaro, Bunu Umi Omar, Theoneste Hakizimana, Nwanganga Ihuoma Ubosi, Zeinab Adam Abakar Mohamed, Chima Paul Okechukwu Ugwu, Emeka Anyanwu, Umi Omar Bunu
Original abstract (English)
Background: Recurrent Ebola virus disease (EVD) outbreaks in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) continue to pose substantial cross-border health security risks to neighbouring countries, particularly Uganda. Although numerous preparedness initiatives have been implemented to prevent, detect, and respond to cross-border Ebola transmission, evidence on their effectiveness, strengths, and persistent gaps remains fragmented across peer-reviewed and grey-literature sources. This review synthesized available evidence on cross-border Ebola preparedness in Uganda and the DRC and identified key capacities, challenges, and priorities for strengthening regional health security. Methods A systematic review was conducted in accordance with PRISMA 2020 and reported using the Synthesis Without Meta-analysis (SWiM) framework. PubMed, Scopus, Web of Science, African Journals Online (AJOL), and major grey-literature sources were searched from January 2000 to 18 May 2026. Findings were synthesized across nine predefined preparedness domains. Results Seventy-one evidence sources were included, comprising 32 primary studies and 39 grey-literature documents. Evidence was most concentrated in border surveillance and point-of-entry preparedness (24 sources), contact tracing and rapid response (22 sources), cross-border mobility and importation risk (18 sources), infection prevention and control (17 sources), and regional governance and coordination (17 sources). Preparedness activities included mobility mapping, traveller screening, healthcare-worker training, laboratory strengthening, risk communication, and cross-border coordination mechanisms. The synthesis identified substantial preparedness capacity across surveillance systems, laboratory readiness, healthcare-facility preparedness, and regional collaboration. However, important gaps persisted in some major preparedness domains. Conclusions Cross-border Ebola preparedness in Uganda and the DRC has expanded considerably over the past two decades through investments in surveillance systems, laboratory networks, healthcare-worker preparedness, and regional coordination. Nonetheless, persistent vulnerabilities remain in humanitarian settings, preparedness sustainability, and emerging preparedness domains. Strengthening integrated, sustainable, and cross-border health-security systems will be critical for mitigating future Ebola threats and enhancing epidemic preparedness across East Africa.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
This review focuses on cross-border Ebola virus disease (EVD) preparedness in Uganda and the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC). The central question is to evaluate the health security capacities, gaps, and priorities for preventing, detecting, and responding to cross-border Ebola transmission between these two countries.
Evidence base and method
A systematic review was conducted in accordance with PRISMA 2020 and reported using the Synthesis Without Meta-analysis (SWiM) framework. The evidence sources were identified through searches of PubMed, Scopus, Web of Science, African Journals Online (AJOL), and major grey-literature sources from January 2000 to 18 May 2026. Findings were synthesized across nine predefined preparedness domains.
Consensus and conclusions
The synthesis identified substantial preparedness capacity across surveillance systems, laboratory readiness, healthcare-facility preparedness, and regional collaboration. Cross-border Ebola preparedness in Uganda and the DRC has expanded considerably over the past two decades through investments in these areas.
Controversies and gaps
Important gaps persisted in some major preparedness domains. Persistent vulnerabilities remain in humanitarian settings, preparedness sustainability, and emerging preparedness domains. The evidence on the effectiveness of preparedness initiatives remains fragmented across peer-reviewed and grey-literature sources.
Research and practice implications
Strengthening integrated, sustainable, and cross-border health-security systems will be critical for mitigating future Ebola threats and enhancing epidemic preparedness across East Africa. Preparedness activities included mobility mapping, traveller screening, healthcare-worker training, laboratory strengthening, risk communication, and cross-border coordination mechanisms.

人类-动物-环境界面的 埃博拉病毒病:对近期 暴发、传播 动态和未来预防策略的范围界定审查

Ebola virus disease at the human–animal–environment interface: a scoping review of recent outbreaks, transmission dynamics, and future preventive strategies
作者： Mohan Bilikallahalli Sannathimmappa, Vinod Nambiar
摘要中文翻译埃博拉病毒病 (EVD) 仍然是最致命的人畜共患出血热之一,病死率 (CFR) 为 25% 至 90% 以上。2018年以来,历次重大暴发;刚果民主共和国(DRC)第10次疫情(2018-2020年)、2021年几内亚疫情死灰复燃、乌干达苏丹埃博拉病毒暴发(2022年、2025年)、刚果民主共和国第16次(开赛)暴发(2025年)以及2026年本迪布焦病毒紧急情况宣布为国际公共卫生紧急事件人们的担忧已经夺去了数千人的生命,凸显了埃博拉病毒病对人类、动物和环境界面的持续威胁以及严重的准备差距。本范围界定综述综合了有关 EVD 暴发 动态、人畜共患 传播、基因组 流行病学、治疗和 疫苗 发展以及 暴发 的社会文化和生态驱动因素的最新证据。继 PRISMA-ScR 之后,我们检索了 PubMed、Embase、Cochrane 图书馆、Web of Science 和 Google Scholar(启动至 2026 年 5 月),并辅以 WHO 暴发 报告。其中包括 50 个符合条件的来源,其中超过 90% 于 2018-2026 年发表。刚果民主共和国第 10 次 暴发(3,470 例;CFR 66.2%)因武装冲突、院内 传播 和社区抵抗而加剧。2021 年几内亚的复苏与 2014 年至 2016 年的幸存者存在基因关联,揭示了病毒在免疫豁免组织中的持续存在。2022 年乌干达 暴发(164 例;病死率 46.3%)强调了私人设施的超级传播和感染控制不足。食果翼蝠科蝙蝠仍然可能是 储存宿主。批准的单克隆抗体 Inmazeb 和 Ebanga 的 28 天 死亡率 分别为 33.5% 和 35.1%,而 ZMapp 为 49.7%;rVSV-ZEBOV 可预防扎伊尔埃博拉病毒,但不能预防苏丹埃博拉病毒。最近发生的事件强化了这些主题:2025 年乌干达(14 例;CFR 29%)将进行首次 SUDV 环 疫苗接种 试验;2025 年 DRC-Kasai(64 例;CFR 70.3%)在约 3 个月内得到控制;2026年Bundibugyo再次暴露了针对非扎伊尔物种缺乏对策的情况。综合 One Health 监测、公平的治疗和 疫苗 获取、社区参与和基因组 流行病学 必须成为未来埃博拉病毒病预防的基础。对苏丹埃博拉病毒 疫苗 和 暴发 具有复原力的卫生系统进行紧急投资至关重要。这是第一次全面的范围界定审查,将 2018 年埃博拉病毒病 暴发 后所有三个主要疫情(即刚果第十次疫情、2021 年几内亚疫情和 2022 年乌干达苏丹埃博拉病毒 暴发)的证据整合到统一的 One Health 框架内。我们提供了基因组 流行病学 以及临床、生态和社会文化 传播 决定因素的新颖合成,弥合了实验室科学和公共卫生实践之间的差距。该审查对 FDA 批准的治疗药物(Inmazeb、Ebanga)和 疫苗 策略(rVSV-ZEBOV)进行了严格的评估,该策略是在目前尚无批准的医疗对策由非扎伊尔埃博拉病毒引起的 暴发 的背景下进行的。我们的分析强调了范式转变的发现,即埃博拉病毒病可以通过扎伊尔埃博拉病毒在幸存者免疫特权组织中的持续存在,在流行病多年后重新出现,这对长期的 监测 政策有直接影响。

范围与问题
埃博拉病毒病 (EVD) 仍然是最致命的人畜共患出血热之一,病死率 (CFR) 为 25% 至 90% 以上。
证据基础与方法
继 PRISMA-ScR 之后,我们检索了 PubMed、Embase、Cochrane 图书馆、Web of Science 和 Google Scholar(启动至 2026 年 5 月),并辅以 WHO 暴发 报告。
共识与结论
批准的单克隆抗体 Inmazeb 和 Ebanga 的 28 天 死亡率 分别为 33.5% 和 35.1%,而 ZMapp 为 49.7%;rVSV-ZEBOV 可预防扎伊尔埃博拉病毒,但不能预防苏丹埃博拉病毒。
争议与缺口
最近发生的事件强化了这些主题:2025 年乌干达(14 例;CFR 29%)将进行首次 SUDV 环 疫苗接种 试验;2025 年 DRC-Kasai(64 例;CFR 70.3%)在约 3 个月内得到控制;2026年Bundibugyo再次暴露了针对非扎伊尔物种缺乏对策的情况。
科研与实践启示
综合 One Health 监测、公平的治疗和 疫苗 获取、社区参与和基因组 流行病学 必须成为未来埃博拉病毒病预防的基础。

Ebola virus disease at the human–animal–environment interface: a scoping review of recent outbreaks, transmission dynamics, and future preventive strategies

Authors: Mohan Bilikallahalli Sannathimmappa, Vinod Nambiar
Original abstract (English)
Ebola Virus Disease (EVD) remains among the most lethal zoonotic hemorrhagic fevers, with case fatality rates (CFRs) of 25% to over 90%. Since 2018, successive major outbreaks; the 10th Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) epidemic (2018–2020), the 2021 Guinea resurgence, the Uganda Sudan ebolavirus outbreaks (2022, 2025), the 16th DRC (Kasai) outbreak (2025), and the 2026 Bundibugyo virus emergency declared a Public Health Emergency of International Concern have claimed thousands of lives, underscoring EVD’s persistent threat at the human–animal–environment interface and critical preparedness gaps. This scoping review synthesizes recent evidence on EVD outbreak dynamics, zoonotic transmission, genomic epidemiology, therapeutic and vaccine developments, and the sociocultural and ecological drivers of outbreaks. Following PRISMA-ScR, we searched PubMed, Embase, Cochrane Library, Web of Science, and Google Scholar (inception to May 2026), supplemented by WHO outbreak reports. Fifty eligible sources were included, over 90% published 2018–2026. The DRC 10th outbreak (3,470 cases; CFR 66.2%) was exacerbated by armed conflict, nosocomial transmission, and community resistance. The 2021 Guinea resurgence was genetically linked to a 2014–2016 survivor, revealing viral persistence in immune-privileged tissues. The 2022 Uganda outbreak (164 cases; CFR 46.3%) highlighted super-spreading and inadequate infection control at private facilities. Frugivorous Pteropodidae bats remain the likely reservoir. Approved monoclonal antibodies Inmazeb and Ebanga showed 28-day mortality of 33.5% and 35.1% versus 49.7% for ZMapp; rVSV-ZEBOV protects against Zaire but not Sudan ebolavirus. Recent events reinforced these themes: 2025 Uganda (14 cases; CFR 29%) saw the first SUDV ring-vaccination trial; 2025 DRC-Kasai (64 cases; CFR 70.3%) was contained within ~ 3 months; and 2026 Bundibugyo again exposed absent countermeasures for non-Zaire species. Integrated One Health surveillance, equitable therapeutic and vaccine access, community engagement, and genomic epidemiology must anchor future EVD prevention. Urgent investment in Sudan ebolavirus vaccines and outbreak-resilient health systems is essential. This is the first comprehensive scoping review to integrate evidence from all three major post-2018 EVD outbreaks namely the DRC 10th epidemic, the 2021 Guinea resurgence, and the 2022 Uganda Sudan ebolavirus outbreak within a unified One Health framework. We provide a novel synthesis of genomic epidemiology alongside clinical, ecological, and sociocultural transmission determinants, bridging the gap between laboratory science and public health practice. The review critically appraises FDA-approved therapeutics (Inmazeb, Ebanga) and vaccine strategies (rVSV-ZEBOV) within the context of ongoing outbreaks caused by non-Zaire ebolaviruses for which no approved medical countermeasures currently exist. Our analysis highlights the paradigm-shifting finding that EVD can re-emerge years after an epidemic through persistence of Zaire ebolavirus in survivor immune-privileged tissues, with direct implications for long-term surveillance policies.

Consensus and conclusions
Approved monoclonal antibodies Inmazeb and Ebanga showed 28-day mortality of 33.5% and 35.1% versus 49.7% for ZMapp; rVSV-ZEBOV protects against Zaire but not Sudan ebolavirus.
Controversies and gaps
Recent events reinforced these themes: 2025 Uganda (14 cases; CFR 29%) saw the first SUDV ring-vaccination trial; 2025 DRC-Kasai (64 cases; CFR 70.3%) was contained within ~ 3 months; and 2026 Bundibugyo again exposed absent countermeasures for non-Zaire species.
Research and practice implications
Integrated One Health surveillance, equitable therapeutic and vaccine access, community engagement, and genomic epidemiology must anchor future EVD prevention.

本迪布焦病毒病:传播 动力学、传染性和病毒持久性。

Bundibugyo virus disease: transmission dynamics, infectiousness and viral persistence.
作者： Andrea Bongiovanni, Erica Binetti, Francesca Colavita, Ilaria Mussetto, Laura Scorzolini, Eleonora Lalle, Andrea Antinori, Enrico Girardi, Fabrizio Maggi, Emanuele Nicastri
摘要中文翻译BVD 是一种由本迪布焦病毒 (BDBV) 引起的严重丝状病毒性出血热,与扎伊尔埃博拉病毒相比,暴发 相关证据仍然有限。该观点总结了有关 传播 动态、症状前传染性、恢复后病毒持续性和传染性持续时间的当前证据,将 BDBV 特异性数据与从其他正埃博拉病毒推断的证据区分开来。乌干达 2007-2008 年 暴发 的现有流行病学数据表明,BDBV 传播 主要通过直接接触有症状或死亡个体的血液或体液而发生,尸体的处理是一个显着的危险因素(调整比值比 3.83;95% 置信区间 1.78-8.23)。平均潜伏期为 6.3 天,在家庭和医疗机构中记录了 传播 的延长链。尚未报告有症状前 传播 的人类病例,但是,记录的 暴发 数量有限,无法排除这种可能性,并且实验动物数据支持其生物学合理性。目前还没有关于病毒持久性和传染性持续时间的 BDBV 特异性数据。因此,目前的建议主要依赖于其他正埃博拉病毒的证据。病毒RNA在免疫豁免部位恢复后可能持续存在,特别是在精液中,罕见但有记录的延迟传播发作。正在进行的 暴发 强调需要进行针对 BDBV 的研究,以加强支持公共卫生建议的证据基础。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
该综述检查了本迪布焦病毒病 (BVD),这是一种由本迪布焦病毒 (BDBV) 引起的严重丝状病毒性出血热,重点关注 传播 动态、症状前传染性、恢复后病毒持续性和传染性持续时间,核心问题是 BDBV 特异性证据与其他正埃博拉病毒的外推数据。
证据基础与方法
该观点综合了 BDBV 的当前证据,将 BDBV 特异性数据与其他正埃博拉病毒的推断证据区分开来,并报告说,与扎伊尔埃博拉病毒相比,BVD 的 暴发 相关证据仍然有限。
共识与结论
2007-2008 年乌干达 暴发 的流行病学数据表明,BDBV 传播 主要通过直接接触有症状或死亡个体的血液或体液而发生,尸体处理是一个重要的危险因素(调整后的比值比 3.83;95% CI 1.78-8.23)。平均潜伏期为 6.3 天,在家庭和医疗机构中记录了 传播 链延长的情况。
争议与缺口
尚未报告 BDBV 出现症状前的 传播 人类病例,但记录的 暴发 数量有限,无法排除这种可能性,而且实验动物数据表明生物学上的合理性。此外,关于病毒持久性和传染性持续时间,不存在 BDBV 特异性数据,目前的建议主要依赖于其他正埃博拉病毒的证据。
科研与实践启示
该审查强调迫切需要针对 BDBV 进行研究,以加强支持公共卫生建议的证据基础,特别是在症状前 传播、病毒持续性和传染性持续时间方面。

Bundibugyo virus disease: transmission dynamics, infectiousness and viral persistence.

Authors: Andrea Bongiovanni, Erica Binetti, Francesca Colavita, Ilaria Mussetto, Laura Scorzolini, Eleonora Lalle, Andrea Antinori, Enrico Girardi, Fabrizio Maggi, Emanuele Nicastri
Original abstract (English)
BVD is a severe filoviral haemorrhagic fever caused by the Bundibugyo virus (BDBV), for which outbreak-related evidence remains limited compared with that available for Zaire ebolavirus. This perspective summarizes current evidence on transmission dynamics, presymptomatic infectiousness, post-recovery viral persistence and the duration of infectiousness, distinguishing BDBV-specific data from evidence extrapolated from other ortho-Ebolaviruses. Available epidemiological data from the 2007-2008 outbreak in Uganda indicate that BDBV transmission occurs primarily through direct contact with the blood or body fluids of symptomatic or deceased individuals, with the handling of corpses representing a significant risk factor (adjusted odds ratio 3.83; 95% confidence interval 1.78-8.23). The average incubation period is 6.3 days, and prolonged chains of transmission were documented in household and healthcare settings. No documented human cases of pre-symptomatic transmission have been reported, however, the limited number of documented outbreaks precludes excluding this possibility, and experimental animal data support its biological plausibility. No BDBV-specific data are available regarding viral persistence and duration of infectiousness. Consequently, current recommendations rely largely on evidence derived from other orthoebolaviruses. Viral RNA may persist after recovery in immunoprivileged sites, particularly in semen, with rare but documented episodes of delayed transmission. The ongoing outbreak highlights the need for BDBV-specific studies to strengthen the evidence base underpinning public health recommendations.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The review examines Bundibugyo virus disease (BVD), a severe filoviral haemorrhagic fever caused by Bundibugyo virus (BDBV), focusing on transmission dynamics, presymptomatic infectiousness, post-recovery viral persistence, and duration of infectiousness, with a central question on BDBV-specific evidence versus extrapolated data from other orthoebolaviruses.
Evidence base and method
This perspective synthesizes current evidence on BDBV, distinguishing BDBV-specific data from extrapolated evidence from other orthoebolaviruses, and reports that outbreak-related evidence for BVD remains limited compared with Zaire ebolavirus.
Consensus and conclusions
Epidemiological data from the 2007-2008 Uganda outbreak indicate that BDBV transmission occurs primarily through direct contact with blood or body fluids of symptomatic or deceased individuals, with corpse handling as a significant risk factor (adjusted odds ratio 3.83; 95% CI 1.78-8.23). The average incubation period is 6.3 days, and prolonged transmission chains were documented in household and healthcare settings.
Controversies and gaps
No documented human cases of presymptomatic transmission have been reported for BDBV, though the limited number of documented outbreaks precludes excluding this possibility, and experimental animal data suggest biological plausibility. Additionally, no BDBV-specific data exist regarding viral persistence and duration of infectiousness, with current recommendations relying largely on evidence from other orthoebolaviruses.
Research and practice implications
The review highlights the urgent need for BDBV-specific studies to strengthen the evidence base underpinning public health recommendations, particularly regarding presymptomatic transmission, viral persistence, and duration of infectiousness.

南苏丹、乌干达和刚果民主共和国埃博拉病毒防范和应对中的水、环境卫生和个人卫生:范围界定审查

Water, Sanitation and Hygiene in Ebola Preparedness and Response in South Sudan, Uganda and the Democratic Republic of the Congo: A Scoping Review
作者： James Arike Charles Duku
摘要中文翻译水、环境卫生和个人卫生 (WASH) 服务是 埃博拉病毒病 暴发 期间感染预防和控制的基础。然而,它们对 暴发 准备和响应的贡献仍未得到充分综合,特别是在脆弱和容易出现 暴发 的环境中。本次范围界定审查探讨了 WASH 在乌干达、刚果民主共和国 (DRC) 和南苏丹埃博拉病毒防范和应对中的重要性。该审查遵循系统审查的首选报告项目和范围审查的荟萃分析扩展 (PRISMA-ScR)。通过 PubMed、African Index Medicus、Google Scholar、African Journals Online (AJOL) 和世界卫生组织信息共享机构存储库 (IRIS) 检索 2014 年至 2026 年间发表的文献。我们考虑了有关这三个国家的 WASH、感染预防和控制、埃博拉病毒准备或应对的同行评审和灰色文献来源。证据按主题绘制和综合。搜索显示了 1,963 条结果,其中 861 条被捕获用于标题、摘要、摘要或全文筛选。经过资格评估和跨平台重复数据删除后,41 个独特来源被纳入其中。出现了六个主题:WASH 基础设施和服务准备情况;感染预防和控制准备;医护人员的准备;社区参与和风险沟通;跨境监测和机构协调;以及体制、财务和后勤方面的限制。可靠的供水、卫生设施、洗手站、环境清洁和废物管理系统支持有效的感染控制实践。然而,由于基础设施不足、防护用品不足、实践培训有限、维护系统薄弱以及对临时外部援助的依赖,准备工作受到削弱。乌干达表现出相对较强的 监测 和快速反应安排;刚果民主共和国拥有丰富的 暴发 经验,但设施层面持续存在弱点,而南苏丹则面临严重的结构性和人道主义限制。可持续的埃博拉防备需要将 WASH 基础设施、实用的医护人员培训、社区参与和跨境协调纳入常规卫生系统规划和融资。需要进行进一步的比较和针对具体国家的研究,以衡量 WASH 干预措施的独立贡献和长期功能。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
该审查审查了乌干达、刚果民主共和国 (DRC) 和南苏丹的 暴发 或 暴发 (EVD) 期间水、环境卫生和个人卫生 (WASH) 服务在感染预防和控制中的作用,重点关注脆弱和 暴发 易发环境的准备和应对。
证据基础与方法
本次范围界定审查遵循 PRISMA-ScR 指南,并使用 PubMed、African Index Medicus、Google Scholar、African Journals Online (AJOL) 和 WHO IRIS 存储库检索了 2014 年至 2026 年期间发表的文献。有关这三个国家的 WASH、感染预防和控制、埃博拉病毒准备或应对的同行评审和灰色文献来源均符合资格。经过重复数据删除和资格评估后,41 个独特的来源被纳入并按主题进行综合。
共识与结论
可靠的供水、卫生设施、洗手站、环境清洁和废物管理系统始终被认为支持埃博拉 暴发 期间有效的感染控制实践。乌干达表现出相对较强的 监测 和快速响应安排,而刚果民主共和国表现出丰富的 暴发 经验,但设施层面持续存在弱点。南苏丹面临严重的结构性和人道主义限制,阻碍了准备工作。
争议与缺口
由于基础设施不足、防护用品不足、实践培训有限、维护系统薄弱以及依赖临时外部援助,准备工作受到削弱。该审查强调需要进一步进行比较和针对具体国家的研究,以衡量 WASH 干预措施的独立贡献和长期功能。
科研与实践启示
可持续的埃博拉防备需要将 WASH 基础设施、实用的医护人员培训、社区参与和跨境协调纳入常规卫生系统规划和融资。加强维护系统和减少对临时外部援助的依赖是关键优先事项。

Water, Sanitation and Hygiene in Ebola Preparedness and Response in South Sudan, Uganda and the Democratic Republic of the Congo: A Scoping Review

Authors: James Arike Charles Duku
Original abstract (English)
Water, sanitation and hygiene (WASH) services are fundamental to infection prevention and control during Ebola virus disease outbreaks. However, their contribution to outbreak preparedness and response remains insufficiently synthesised, particularly in fragile and outbreak-prone settings. This scoping review examined the importance of WASH in Ebola preparedness and response in Uganda, the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) and South Sudan. The review followed the Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyses Extension for Scoping Reviews (PRISMA-ScR). Literature published between 2014 and 2026 was searched through PubMed, African Index Medicus, Google Scholar, African Journals Online (AJOL) and the World Health Organization Institutional Repository for Information Sharing (IRIS). Peer-reviewed and grey-literature sources addressing WASH, infection prevention and control, preparedness or response to Ebola in the three countries were considered. The evidence was charted and synthesised thematically. The searches displayed 1,963 results, of which 861 were captured for title, abstract, summary or full-text screening. After eligibility assessment and cross-platform deduplication, 41 unique sources were included. Six themes emerged: WASH infrastructure and service readiness; infection prevention and control preparedness; healthcare-worker preparedness; community engagement and risk communication; cross-border surveillance and institutional coordination; and institutional, financial and logistical constraints. Reliable water supplies, sanitation facilities, handwashing stations, environmental cleaning and waste-management systems supported effective infection-control practices. Nevertheless, preparedness was weakened by inadequate infrastructure, insufficient protective supplies, limited practical training, weak maintenance systems and dependence on temporary external assistance. Uganda demonstrated comparatively stronger surveillance and rapid-response arrangements; the DRC had extensive outbreak experience but persistent facility-level weaknesses, while South Sudan faced substantial structural and humanitarian constraints. Sustainable Ebola preparedness requires WASH infrastructure, practical healthcare-worker training, community participation and cross-border coordination to be incorporated into routine health-system planning and financing. Further comparative and country-specific research is required to measure the independent contribution and long-term functionality of WASH interventions.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The review examines the role of water, sanitation and hygiene (WASH) services in infection prevention and control during outbreaks of Ebola virus disease (EVD) in Uganda, the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) and South Sudan, focusing on preparedness and response in fragile and outbreak-prone settings.
Evidence base and method
This scoping review followed the PRISMA-ScR guidelines and searched literature published between 2014 and 2026 using PubMed, African Index Medicus, Google Scholar, African Journals Online (AJOL) and the WHO IRIS repository. Peer-reviewed and grey-literature sources addressing WASH, infection prevention and control, preparedness or response to Ebola in the three countries were eligible. After deduplication and eligibility assessment, 41 unique sources were included and synthesised thematically.
Consensus and conclusions
Reliable water supplies, sanitation facilities, handwashing stations, environmental cleaning and waste-management systems are consistently identified as supporting effective infection-control practices during Ebola outbreaks. Uganda demonstrated comparatively stronger surveillance and rapid-response arrangements, while the DRC showed extensive outbreak experience but persistent facility-level weaknesses. South Sudan faced substantial structural and humanitarian constraints that impeded preparedness.
Controversies and gaps
Preparedness was weakened by inadequate infrastructure, insufficient protective supplies, limited practical training, weak maintenance systems and dependence on temporary external assistance. The review highlights the need for further comparative and country-specific research to measure the independent contribution and long-term functionality of WASH interventions.
Research and practice implications
Sustainable Ebola preparedness requires integrating WASH infrastructure, practical healthcare-worker training, community participation and cross-border coordination into routine health-system planning and financing. Strengthening maintenance systems and reducing reliance on temporary external assistance are critical priorities.

新闻简报本报告详细介绍了刚果民主共和国 (DRC) 的本迪布焦病毒病 暴发,该病主要影响伊图里和北基伍地区。非洲疾病预防控制中心2026年8月11日发布的情况报告强调,刚果民主共和国病死率高达45.5%,其中99.5%的病例和99.9%的死亡发生在刚果民主共和国。暴发 仍在持续,传播 持续走高。该报告没有具体说明测试或接触者追踪等详细的应对措施,但强调了 暴发 的严重性以及进一步传播的风险。报告引用的数据截至2026年8月2日,报告基于部分原始文章正文。
查看新闻五要素
时间
The outbreak is reported as of August 5, 2026, with the situation report published on August 11, 2026. The data referenced in the report is as of 02 August 2026.
地点与对象
暴发 主要位于刚果民主共和国 (DRC),特别是伊图里和北基伍地区。受影响的人群包括人类,没有提及动物 宿主。
事件
据报道,本迪布焦病毒病 暴发 病死率高达 45.5%。该报告由非洲疾病预防控制中心发布,表明受影响地区的 传播 持续走高。
规模、影响与风险
暴发的病死率高达45.5%,其中99.5%的病例和99.9%的死亡集中在刚果民主共和国。该报告强调了 暴发 的严重性以及伊图里和北基伍地区持续高水平 传播 的风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
情况报告显示,暴发正在非洲疾控中心的积极监测下。该报告是对当前状态的总结,并未提供具体应对措施的详细信息,例如测试、隔离或接触者追踪。

Bundibugyo Virus Disease Outbreak | Situation Report No. 80 – August 5, 2026 - ReliefWeb

News Brief
This report details a Bundibugyo virus disease outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), primarily affecting Ituri and North-Kivu regions. The situation report, issued by Africa CDC on August 11, 2026, highlights a high case-fatality ratio of 45.5%, with 99.5% of cases and 99.9% of deaths occurring in the DRC. The outbreak is ongoing, with continued high transmission noted. The report does not specify detailed response measures such as testing or contact tracing, but it underscores the severity of the outbreak and the risk of further spread. The data referenced in the report is as of August 2, 2026, and the report is based on a partial original article body.

Bundibugyo Virus Disease Outbreak | Situation Report No. 79 – August 4, 2026 - ReliefWeb

News Brief
The Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) is experiencing a severe Bundibugyo Virus Disease outbreak, with 99.5% of global cases and 99.9% of deaths occurring within its borders, according to the latest situation report covering 27 July to 2 August 2026. The outbreak is concentrated in Ituri and North-Kivu provinces, where transmission remains high. The case-fatality ratio stands at 45.2%, underscoring critical delays in access to care. The report, issued by Africa CDC and published by ReliefWeb on 11 August 2026, highlights the ongoing public health challenge despite the absence of detailed response measures in the available summary.

IOM:多国 Bundibugyo 埃博拉病毒病 暴发 - 第 12 号情况报告(2026 年 7 月 27 日至 8 月 2 日) - ReliefWeb

IOM: Multi-Country Bundibugyo Ebola Virus Disease Outbreak - Situation Report No. 12 (27 July - 2 August 2026) - ReliefWeb
新闻简报The World Health Organization reports the Democratic Republic of the Congo’s Bundibugyo Ebola virus disease outbreak has reached 3,532 confirmed cases and 1,556 deaths as of 29 July 2026, making it the largest and fastest-growing Bundibugyo Ebola outbreak on record in the country. The epidemic is expanding rapidly into Tshopo and Haut-Uélé provinces, raising cross-border risks for South Sudan and the Central African Republic. Against a backdrop of persistent conflict, large-scale displacement, and insecurity, most deaths continue to occur in communities, underscoring gaps in contact tracing and event-based surveillance. The International Organization for Migration has screened 1.4 million people in DRC during the past week and over 12.2 million at 223 points of entry across the region since the response began, while reaching more than 10.7 million with risk communication. Uganda declared its outbreak over on 28 July, but regional preparedness and post-outbreak monitoring remain active.
查看新闻五要素
时间
活动时间:2026年7月27日-8月2日(报告周);发布日期:2026 年 8 月 7 日。
地点与对象
刚果民主共和国(DRC),包括东部省份(乔波省、上韦莱省)和金沙萨;该地区面临风险和受影响的国家;难民营中的流离失所者。
事件
世卫组织确认的 Bundibugyo 埃博拉病毒病 (BVD) 暴发 据 IOM 报告为刚果民主共和国最大且增长最快的 BVD 暴发;情况报告编号
规模、影响与风险
截至2026年7月29日,确诊BVD病例3,532例,死亡1,556例;迅速向乔波和上韦莱地区扩张;跨境传播至南苏丹和中非共和国的风险;持续的不安全、流离失所和人口流动使控制变得更加复杂。
应对、状态与不确定性
IOM conducted 1.4 million health screenings in DRC during the reporting period; over 12.2 million screenings at 223 points of entry across affected and at-risk countries since the start of the response; 10.7 million people reached with risk communication and community engagement; contact tracing and community event-based surveillance remain insufficient; Uganda declared the end of its BVD outbreak on 28 July 2026 but regional preparedness continues; Kinshasa alert involving a vessel with ~200 passengers and a probable BVD case under investigation.

IOM: Multi-Country Bundibugyo Ebola Virus Disease Outbreak - Situation Report No. 12 (27 July - 2 August 2026) - ReliefWeb

News Brief
The World Health Organization reports the Democratic Republic of the Congo’s Bundibugyo Ebola virus disease outbreak has reached 3,532 confirmed cases and 1,556 deaths as of 29 July 2026, making it the largest and fastest-growing Bundibugyo Ebola outbreak on record in the country. The epidemic is expanding rapidly into Tshopo and Haut-Uélé provinces, raising cross-border risks for South Sudan and the Central African Republic. Against a backdrop of persistent conflict, large-scale displacement, and insecurity, most deaths continue to occur in communities, underscoring gaps in contact tracing and event-based surveillance. The International Organization for Migration has screened 1.4 million people in DRC during the past week and over 12.2 million at 223 points of entry across the region since the response began, while reaching more than 10.7 million with risk communication. Uganda declared its outbreak over on 28 July, but regional preparedness and post-outbreak monitoring remain active.

Search|t Updates Countries Disasters Organizations Topics Jobs Training Informing humanitarians worldwide 24/7 — a service provided by UN OCHA DR Congo + 10 more IOM: Multi-Country Bundibugyo Ebola Virus Disease Outbreak - Situation Report No. 12 (27 July - 2 August 2026) Format Situation Report Source IOM Posted 7 Aug 2026 Originally published 7 Aug 2026 Attachments Download Report (PDF | 3.01 MB) According to the World Health Organization, as of 29 July, the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) reported 3,532 confirmed cases of Ebola disease caused by the Bundibugyo virus (BVD), including 1,556 deaths. With the recent increase in figures, the Ebola Bundibugyo outbreak has become the largest and fastest-growing outbreak in DRC. The rapidly escalating outbreak is unfolding against the backdrop of an ongoing triple crisis in eastern DRC, where health risks, large-scale displacement and persistent conflict intersect. Displaced populations in camps require multisectoral interventions in response to multidimensional needs. Across affected areas, most deaths continue to occur within communities, underscoring the urgent need to strengthen contact tracing and community event-based surveillance. The spread of the outbreak into Tshopo and Haut-Uélé introduce new risks within these provinces, across the country and for the wider region. In Tshopo, patient movement in search of health care is contributing to further transmission, while the rising number of cases in Haut-Uélé requires enhanced disease surveillance and cross-border coordination to prevent disease transmission into neighbouring South Sudan and the Central African Republic (CAR). Key Highlights: The Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) has reported 3,532 confirmed Ebola Bundibugyo cases and 1,556 deaths, making this the largest and fastest-growing BVD outbreak recorded in the country. IOM has supported over 12.2 million health screenings at 223 points of entry and points of control across affected and at-risk countries. More than 10.7 million people have been reached through risk communication and community engagement activities. During the reporting period, IOM supported 1.4 million health screenings in DRC, activating additional screening locations in response to the outbreak's geographic expansion. Uganda officially declared the end of its BVD outbreak on 28 July 2026, but preparedness and post-outbreak monitoring activities continue given the persistent regional risk. IOM is rapidly expanding surveillance and response efforts in Kinshasa, including support following an alert involving a vessel carrying approximately 200 passengers and a probable Ebola case. The outbreak continues to unfold in a complex operational environment marked by insecurity, displacement, and population mobility. As transmission persists in eastern DRC, sustained investment in surveillance, border health measures, community engagement, and support to displaced and mobile populations remains critical to containing the outbreak and preventing cross-border spread. Scale, impact and risk
3,532 confirmed BVD cases and 1,556 deaths (as of 29 July 2026); rapid geographic expansion into Tshopo and Haut-Uélé; risk of cross-border spread to South Sudan and Central African Republic; ongoing insecurity, displacement, and population mobility complicate control.
Response, status and uncertainty
IOM conducted 1.4 million health screenings in DRC during the reporting period; over 12.2 million screenings at 223 points of entry across affected and at-risk countries since the start of the response; 10.7 million people reached with risk communication and community engagement; contact tracing and community event-based surveillance remain insufficient; Uganda declared the end of its BVD outbreak on 28 July 2026 but regional preparedness continues; Kinshasa alert involving a vessel with ~200 passengers and a probable BVD case under investigation.

Ebola Bundibugyo virus disease outbreak: Democratic Republic of the Congo | Uganda Weekly External Situation Report 12, Data as of 02 August 2026 - ReliefWeb

Ebola Bundibugyo virus disease outbreak: Democratic Republic of the Congo | Uganda Weekly External Situation Report 12, Data as of 02 August 2026 - ReliefWeb
新闻简报截至 2026 年 8 月 2 日,刚果民主共和国的本迪布焦病毒病 传播 已成为有记录以来最严重的病例,确诊病例为 18,276 例,死亡人数为 8,214 例。传播 疫情依然严重,集中在伊图里省的布尼亚-鲁万帕拉-蒙布瓦卢-尼兹走廊,近期病例的 84% 都发生在该地区。粗病死率为 44.9%,其中五岁以下儿童受到的影响尤为严重。尽管响应工作不断进行,但 监测 差距仍然存在,仅 79.2% 的接触者在 24 小时内得到追踪。世卫组织警告称,尽管尚未发现新的国际病例,但 暴发 仍处于扩展阶段,并构成区域和国际传播的高风险。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026 年 8 月 6 日发布;截至 2026 年 8 月 2 日的活动数据;每周报告期涵盖2026年7月26日至8月2日。
地点与对象
刚果民主共和国 (DRC):五个省(伊图里、北基伍、上韦莱、乔波、南基伍)设有 51 个卫生区。主要传播走廊:伊图里省的布尼亚-鲁瓦帕拉-蒙布瓦卢-尼济。受影响的人群包括 20-59 岁的成年人(传播 的主要驱动者)和 5 岁以下的儿童(传播 比例过高)。
事件
世卫组织通过 ReliefWeb 情况报告报告了确诊的本迪布焦病毒病 (BVD) 暴发。事件正在持续、确认并扩大,传播 和 死亡率 持续较高。
规模、影响与风险
累计确诊病例:18,276例;累计确诊死亡人数:8,214 人(粗死亡率 44.9%)。上周(2026 年 7 月 26 日至 8 月 2 日)新增 540 例确诊病例和 270 例确诊死亡。过去21天:全国确诊病例1867例,死亡953例;87.9%的病例和84.8%的死亡发生在伊图里省。由于 传播 的持续存在,区域和国际传播的高风险仍然存在。
应对、状态与不确定性
响应包括接触者追踪(18,276 名接触者正在追踪;79.2% 是在过去 24 小时内发现的,低于 95% 的目标)、监测 和病例管理。9个卫生区已经超过3周没有报告病例;传播 可能会被中断,但 监测 灵敏度不确定。延迟病例检测和延迟访问 治疗 仍然是 死亡率 的主要原因。没有发现新的国际传播。
A high priorityPublic-health news

Ebola Bundibugyo virus disease outbreak: Democratic Republic of the Congo | Uganda Weekly External Situation Report 12, Data as of 02 August 2026 - ReliefWeb

News Brief
The Democratic Republic of the Congo’s Bundibugyo virus disease outbreak has become the largest ever recorded, with 18,276 confirmed cases and 8,214 deaths as of 2 August 2026. Transmission remains intense, concentrated in Ituri Province’s Bunia–Rwampara–Mongbwalu–Nizi corridor, where 84% of recent cases occurred. The crude case fatality ratio stands at 44.9%, with children under five disproportionately affected. Despite ongoing response efforts, surveillance gaps persist, with only 79.2% of contacts traced within 24 hours. WHO warns the outbreak remains in an expansion phase and poses a high risk of regional and international spread, though no new international cases have been detected.
Original source text (English)
Skip to main content Help Log in ReliefWeb Search Content Search What are you looking for? Search|t Updates Countries Disasters Organizations Topics Jobs Training Informing humanitarians worldwide 24/7 — a service provided by UN OCHA DR Congo + 2 more Ebola Bundibugyo virus disease outbreak: Democratic Republic of the Congo | Uganda Weekly External Situation Report 12, Data as of 02 August 2026 Format Situation Report Source WHO Posted 6 Aug 2026 Originally published 6 Aug 2026 Origin View original Attachments Download Report (PDF | 1.48 MB) Event description The Bundibugyo virus disease (BVD) outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo continues to expand rapidly, with sustained transmission, persistently high mortality, and ongoing geographic spread. It is now the largest BVD outbreak ever recorded and the second largest Ebola disease outbreak in history after the 2013 – 2016 West Africa epidemic. Since its detection only a few months ago, the epidemic has grown at an exceptional rate, highlighting its intensity and the challenges facing response efforts. Although no new international spread has been detected, continued transmission indicates that the outbreak remains in an expansion phase and continues to pose a high risk of regional and international spread. Democratic Republic of the Congo Since the last update of 26 July 2026 (Weekly External Situation Report #11), the Democratic Republic of the Congo has reported an additional 540 confirmed cases and 270 confirmed deaths, representing increases of 16.6% and 18.8%, respectively, in cumulative confirmed cases and deaths. Although the absolute numbers of newly reported cases and deaths remain substantial, the proportional increases are markedly lower than those reported during the previous reporting period, reflecting the absence of the large retrospective data reconciliation that contributed to last week's increase. Nevertheless, the reporting of more than 500 additional confirmed cases and more than 250 confirmed deaths within a single week indicates that transmission remains intense and that the outbreak continues to expand. The crude case fatality ratio (CFR) increased from 44.1% to 44.9%, reflecting persistently high mortality despite ongoing response efforts. During the reporting period, the cumulative number of affected health zones increased from 48 to 51, with Kabondo and Wanie-Rukula in Tshopo Province and Lubero in North Kivu Province becoming the latest affected health zones, further demonstrating the continued geographic expansion of the outbreak. During the last 21 days, transmission remained highly concentrated geographically, with an additional 1,867 confirmed cases and 953 confirmed deaths reported nationally. Approximately 87.9% (n=1,641) of recently reported confirmed cases and 84.8% (n=808) of deaths occurred in Ituri Province, with the Bunia–Rwampara–Mongbwalu– Nizi corridor continuing to represent the principal transmission focus. North Kivu, Haut-Uélé, and Tshopo together accounted for 223 cases (11.9%) and 144 deaths (15.1%), while South Kivu reported no new confirmed cases. However, an imported confirmed death from Tshopo Province generated new contacts requiring follow-up in South Kivu. Of the 51 affected health zones, 42 reported at least one confirmed case during the preceding 21 days, whereas nine health zones (Gety, Kambala, Lubunga, Mangobo, Mabalako, Makiso-Kisangani, Miti-Murhesa, Rungu, and Vuhovi) have not reported a confirmed case for more than three weeks, suggesting that transmission may have been interrupted in these areas, provided surveillance remains sufficiently sensitive to detect any ongoing transmission. The largest increases in confirmed cases over the previous 21 days were recorded in Bunia (312 cases), Nizi (285), Rwampara (216), Mongbwalu (207), Katwa (104), Lita (84), Nia-Nia (66), Mangala (63), Fataki (31), and Butembo (29). Together, these ten health zones accounted for approximately 84% of all additional confirmed cases reported nationally during the period. Bunia, Nizi, Mongbwalu, and Rwampara remained the principal transmission corridor, accounting for nearly 55% of all newly reported confirmed cases. Continued increases in Lita, Nia-Nia, Mangala, and Fataki indicate sustained transmission within the Ituri epicentre, while ongoing transmission in Katwa and Butembo confirms persistent transmission in North Kivu. Continued increases in Isiro and Wamba further indicate that the outbreak is becoming increasingly established in Haut-Uélé Province. Mortality remained concentrated within the same transmission corridor driving the epidemic. More than half (55.4%) of all confirmed deaths continue to occur outside designated treatment facilities, indicating that many patients are still being detected only late in illness or after death. Delayed case detection and delayed access to specialised treatment therefore remain major contributors to mortality. Recent analyses indicate a median interval of eight days from symptom onset to death, with almost half of deaths occurring within the first week of illness, emphasizing the narrow window available for life-saving clinical intervention. Children younger than five years continue to experience disproportionately high mortality. Although they account for fewer than one in ten confirmed cases nationally, they represent approximately 17% of all confirmed deaths, experience the highest case fatality ratios of any age group, and have the shortest interval between symptom onset and death. Conversely, adults aged 20 – 59 years continue to account for most transmission, representing nearly two-thirds of confirmed cases. These findings suggest that outbreak control should continue to prioritise interrupting transmission among working-age adults while simultaneously strengthening rapid identification and referral of young children to reduce mortality. Surveillance performance remains suboptimal. As of 2 August 2026, 18,276 contacts were under follow-up nationally, of whom 14,466 (79.2%) were successfully seen during the previous 24 hours, remaining well below the operational target of 95%. Contact tracing capacity continues to lag behind the growing number of contacts to follow, particularly in Ituri and North Kivu, while persistently low follow-up rates and intermittent reporting in Haut-Uélé and Tshopo limit interpretation of recent trends. Since the beginning of the outbreak, the Democratic Republic of the Congo has reported 3,802 confirmed cases, including 1,707 confirmed deaths, corresponding to a crude CFR of 44.9%. The outbreak now affects 51 health zones across five provinces. Ituri Province remains the epicentre, accounting for 3,317 confirmed cases (87.2%) and 1,382 confirmed deaths (81.0%) nationwide. The most affected health zones remain Bunia (914 cases, 270 deaths), Rwampara (655 cases, 263 deaths), Mongbwalu (552 cases, 263 deaths), Nizi (402 cases, 194 deaths), Lita (141 cases, 84 deaths), and Nyankunde (115 cases, 34 deaths) in Ituri Province, together with Katwa (186 cases, 122 deaths) and Butembo (87 cases, 69 deaths) in North Kivu Province. Collectively, these health zones account for the overwhelming majority of confirmed cases and deaths reported nationwide. Time
Published 6 August 2026; event data as of 2 August 2026; weekly reporting period covers 26 July–2 August 2026.
Location and population
Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC): 51 health zones across five provinces (Ituri, North Kivu, Haut-Uélé, Tshopo, South Kivu). Principal transmission corridor: Bunia–Rwampara–Mongbwalu–Nizi in Ituri Province. Affected populations include adults aged 20–59 years (major transmission drivers) and children under five years (disproportionately high mortality).
Event
Confirmed Bundibugyo virus disease (BVD) outbreak reported by WHO via ReliefWeb situation report. Event is ongoing, confirmed, and expanding with sustained transmission and persistently high mortality.
Scale, impact and risk
Cumulative confirmed cases: 18,276; cumulative confirmed deaths: 8,214 (crude CFR 44.9%). Additional 540 confirmed cases and 270 confirmed deaths reported in the last week (26 Jul–2 Aug 2026). In the last 21 days: 1,867 confirmed cases and 953 confirmed deaths nationally; 87.9% of cases and 84.8% of deaths occurred in Ituri Province. High risk of regional and international spread remains due to continued transmission.
Response, status and uncertainty
Response includes contact tracing (18,276 contacts under follow-up; 79.2% seen in last 24 hours, below 95% target), surveillance, and case management. Nine health zones have not reported cases for >3 weeks; transmission may be interrupted but surveillance sensitivity is uncertain. Delayed case detection and late access to treatment remain major contributors to mortality. No new international spread detected.

News Brief

Bundibugyo Virus Disease Outbreak | Issue No. 81 - August 6, 2026 – Africa CDC - ReliefWeb

News Brief
The Bundibugyo virus disease outbreak in Uganda was declared over on 28 July 2026 after the final case was confirmed as imported and managed in a controlled setting. All 831 contacts completed the 21-day follow-up, and no new cases, deaths, admissions, or active contacts were reported. The Africa CDC confirmed this status, and the report was originally published on 6 August 2026 and later posted on 11 August 2026. The outbreak is part of a broader context involving DR Congo, with cumulative figures retained for continental reporting. The response included contact tracing and monitoring, but no explicit risk level was stated. The source is a partial content report, and the information is based on official statements from the Africa CDC.

A high priorityPublic-health news

South Sudan: Rapid Ebola Virus Disease Knowledge Assessment in High-Risk Areas, July 2026 - ReliefWeb

News Brief
A July 2026 Rapid Ebola Virus Disease Knowledge Assessment in South Sudan revealed mixed awareness levels. While respondents recognized common EVD symptoms, many in high-risk areas, particularly Juba, exhibited low risk perception despite widespread awareness. Health workers and radio were primary information sources, but remote communities lacked access to these channels. The assessment highlighted critical gaps in public health communication, with respondents expressing a strong desire for additional information. No confirmed outbreaks or cases were reported during the study period. ReliefWeb's full report (N1-N4) underscores the need for targeted education campaigns to address misconceptions and improve risk awareness in vulnerable regions.

Ebola Outbreak: Current Situation - Centers for Disease Control and Prevention | CDC (.gov)
新闻简报美国疾病控制与预防中心 (CDC) 正在应对刚果民主共和国 (DRC) 和乌干达偏远地区由本迪布焦病毒引起的埃博拉病毒 暴发。此次 暴发 疫情始于刚果民主共和国东北部,蔓延至乌干达坎帕拉,是有记录以来的第二大疫情,在响应启动后 40 天内确诊病例数已超过 1,000 例。7 月第一周据报道有 100 多人死亡。美国尚未确诊病例,由于健全的公共卫生系统和感染控制措施,传播到美国的风险仍然较低。由于该地区持续的冲突、对政府缺乏信任以及错误信息,应对措施变得复杂。疾病预防控制中心继续密切监测局势并提供指导以防止进一步传播。
查看新闻五要素
时间
CDC 于 2026 年 8 月 7 日发布了此更新。截至 2026 年 8 月 11 日的 暴发 数据表明这种情况仍在持续。
地点与对象
暴发 发生在刚果民主共和国 (DRC) 和乌干达的偏远地区,特别是刚果民主共和国东北部和乌干达坎帕拉。受影响人群包括这些地区的个人,美国没有确诊病例。
事件
CDC 正在应对刚果民主共和国和乌干达由本迪布焦病毒引起的埃博拉病毒 暴发。暴发 的传播速度比之前更快,是有记录以来第二大的传播速度。美国尚无确诊病例
规模、影响与风险
暴发 响应启动后 40 天内确诊病例数已超过 1,000 例。7 月第一周据报道有 100 多人死亡。美国公众和旅行者面临的风险仍然很低,乌干达没有出现社区传播,美国也没有确诊病例。

CDC正在积极响应暴发,情况正在调查中。由于持续的冲突、对政府缺乏信任以及错误信息,应对措施变得复杂。由于公共卫生措施,传播到美国的风险被认为非常低。
A high priorityPublic-health news

Ebola Outbreak: Current Situation - Centers for Disease Control and Prevention | CDC (.gov)

News Brief
The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) is addressing an Ebola outbreak caused by Bundibugyo virus in remote areas of the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) and Uganda. This outbreak, which began in northeastern DRC and spread to Kampala, Uganda, is the second-largest on record and has surpassed 1,000 confirmed cases within 40 days of response activation. Over 100 fatalities were reported in the first week of July. No cases have been confirmed in the United States, and the risk of spreading to the U.S. remains low due to robust public health systems and infection control measures. The response is complicated by ongoing conflict, lack of trust in government, and misinformation in the region. The CDC continues to monitor the situation closely and provide guidance to prevent further spread.
Original source text (English)

Español Key points CDC is responding to an outbreak of Ebola disease caused by Bundibugyo virus in remote areas of the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) and Uganda. To date, no cases of Ebola disease have been confirmed in the United States because of this outbreak. The overall risk to the American public and travelers remains low. Outbreak overview Risk to the United States To date, no Ebola cases associated with this outbreak have been reported in the United States. The likelihood of Ebola spreading to the United States is considered very low. If a case was diagnosed in the United States, the risk of Ebola spreading in the United States is also low due to the strength of our public health system and infection control measures. CDC is responding to an outbreak of Ebola in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) and Uganda. This outbreak is spreading substantially faster than previous Ebola outbreaks and is now the second-largest Ebola outbreak on record. The outbreak is caused by Bundibugyo virus, a type of Ebola virus, in northeastern DRC and Uganda's capital Kampala. In DRC, cases have been confirmed in Haut-Uele, Ituri, Nord-Kivu (North Kivu), Sud-Kivu (South Kivu), and Tshopo provinces. This is the 17th outbreak of Ebola in DRC. This Ebola outbreak surpassed 1,000 confirmed cases within 40 days of response activation. In comparison, the 2018 Ebola outbreak in DRC took approximately 235 days to reach more than 1,000 cases. South Kivu has reported no confirmed cases since May 29. The number of reported deaths has risen rapidly as well, with more than 100 fatalities reported in the first week of July. All cases in Uganda so far have been diagnosed in its capital of Kampala, with no community spread reported. Uganda's last reported case, confirmed on June 21, was related to travel from DRC. The response to this outbreak is challenging due to a number of complex circumstances in the region. Limited health infrastructure, including limited access to healthcare, diagnostic and laboratory services, infection prevention and control supplies, and other basic services make stopping this outbreak more difficult. Ongoing conflict in the area where the outbreak is occurring creates security issues and complicates contact tracing and infection prevention and control. Violence against healthcare workers and shortages of personal protective equipment are leading to healthcare workers becoming infected while treating patients. Frequent population movement and cross-border travel increase the risk of spread to Uganda, South Sudan, Rwanda, and to other areas. Lack of trust in the government and misinformation make communicating about Ebola more difficult. To reduce the risk of Ebola importation into the United States, U.S. entry for travelers who were recently in the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) is temporarily restricted. Travelers, including Americans, who have been in DRC within 21 days of their flight will not be allowed to board commercial flights with U.S. destinations. All U.S. citizens and U.S. nationals who have been in DRC should plan to remain outside DRC for 21 days before entering the United States. This includes all passengers with an intermediate stop in DRC or on multi-stop itineraries via DRC regardless of whether they disembark or stay on the airplane. U.S. citizens and U.S. nationals who have been present in Uganda or South Sudan (and not present in DRC) within 21 days of arrival in the United States must only enter through designated airports for enhanced screening. This process is in addition to CDC's renewed July 13 order, under 42 CFR 71.40, that continued the temporary entry restrictions to the United States for certain categories of travelers after they were in DRC, Uganda, or South Sudan. CDC recommends avoiding all travel to Ituri and Nord-Kivu (North Kivu) provinces and avoiding nonessential travel to Haut-Uele and Tshopo provinces in DRC. Travelers to other parts of DRC or Uganda should take enhanced precautions to avoid Ebola exposure and monitor for symptoms while traveling and for 21 days after leaving. Latest data Reported cases This is a rapidly evolving situation, and case counts are subject to change. The DRC, Uganda, and France ministries of health (MOH) report the following: Skip Data Table Data table showing data for the table figure. The DRC MoH does not include probable cases in their official reporting. Suspected cases and suspected deaths in DRC have been temporarily excluded from the count pending ongoing investigations and data standardization. The Uganda MoH does not include suspected cases in their official reporting. Map of affected areas View LargerDownload To date, the Ebola disease outbreak in DRC has been confirmed in Haut-Uele, Ituri, Nord-Kivu (North Kivu), Sud-Kivu (South Kivu), and Tshopo provinces. Cases related to the DRC outbreak also have been reported in Uganda's capital of Kampala. CDC response CDC is working internationally and domestically to respond to this outbreak and prevent Ebola from entering the United States. About 400 people at CDC are involved in responding to the outbreak, including more than 120 people deployed to the affected countries. View five news elements
Time
The CDC published this update on August 7, 2026. The outbreak data as of August 11, 2026, indicates the situation is ongoing.
Location and population
The outbreak is occurring in remote areas of the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) and Uganda, specifically in northeastern DRC and Kampala, Uganda. The affected population includes individuals in these regions, with no confirmed cases in the United States.
Event
CDC is responding to an outbreak of Ebola disease caused by Bundibugyo virus in the DRC and Uganda. The outbreak is spreading faster than previous ones and is the second-largest on record. No confirmed cases in the U.S.
Scale, impact and risk
The outbreak has surpassed 1,000 confirmed cases within 40 days of response activation. Over 100 fatalities were reported in the first week of July. The risk to the American public and travelers remains low, with no community spread in Uganda and no confirmed cases in the U.S.
Response, status and uncertainty
The CDC is actively responding to the outbreak, and the situation is under investigation. The response is complicated by ongoing conflict, lack of trust in government, and misinformation. The risk of spreading to the U.S. is considered very low due to public health measures.

Español What to know Travel from Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC): Travelers, including U.S. citizens and U.S. nationals, who have been in DRC within 21 days before departure will not be allowed to board commercial flights with U.S. destinations. These travelers should plan to remain outside the United States for at least 21 days after they leave DRC. If they have been in outbreak-affected provinces in DRC, they may be subject to additional travel requirements from local authorities before they leave DRC. Travel from Uganda or South Sudan: U.S. citizens and U.S. nationals who have been present in Uganda or South Sudan (and not present in DRC) within 21 days before arrival in the United States will have their air travel routed through designated U.S. airports for public health entry screening. Background To reduce the risk of Ebola importation into the United States, U.S. entry for travelers who were recently in the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) is temporarily restricted. Travelers, including Americans, who have been in DRC within 21 days of their flight will not be allowed to board commercial flights with U.S. destinations. All U.S. citizens and U.S. nationals who have been in DRC should plan to remain outside DRC for 21 days before entering the United States. This includes all passengers with an intermediate stop in DRC or on multi-stop itineraries via DRC regardless of whether they disembark or stay on the airplane. U.S. citizens and U.S. nationals who have been present in Uganda or South Sudan (and not present in DRC) within 21 days of arrival in the United States must only enter through designated airports for enhanced screening. This process is in addition to CDC's renewed July 13 order, under 42 CFR 71.40, that continued the temporary entry restrictions to the United States for certain categories of travelers after they were in DRC, Uganda, or South Sudan. More information for travelers Americans who need consular assistance can visit Additional Consular Information for Americans Regarding the Ebola Outbreak or call the Department of State: From U.S. and Canada: +1-888-407-4747 All other locations: +1-202-501-4444 Keep Reading Order: Suspending the Right to Introduce Certain Persons from Countries Where a Quarantinable Communicable Disease Exists Before you travel to the U.S. Travel from DRC The United States has temporary travel restrictions for people, including U.S. citizens and U.S. nationals, who have recently been in DRC. If you have been in DRC during the previous 21 days: You will not be permitted to board a commercial flight to the United States until 21 days have passed since you left DRC. This applies even if you were on an airplane that stopped at an airport in DRC regardless of whether you disembarked or stayed on the airplane. You should plan to remain outside the United States until at least 21 days have passed since you left DRC. IMPORTANT Travelers who have been in outbreak provinces in the DRC may have specific travel requirements from local DRC authorities. Always check entry requirements of any country you plan to visit during the 21 days after you leave DRC. Use this graphic as a reference for when you can return to the United States. View Larger After leaving the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), travelers must remain outside the United States for 21 days. They may return to the United States beginning on Day 22. Travel from South Sudan or Uganda The United States has temporary travel restrictions for certain travelers, including U.S. lawful permanent residents (Green Card holders), who have recently been in Uganda or South Sudan. Travel by U.S. citizens and nationals who have been in South Sudan or Uganda is not restricted. For travelers who are permitted to enter the United States: Your travel will be rerouted to a designated U.S. airport for public health entry screening. This requirement applies even if you only traveled through South Sudan or Uganda on a connecting flight. Airlines will work with affected travelers to rebook flights, if needed. After You Arrive in the U.S. Travelers who have been in South Sudan or Uganda in the past 21 days and are permitted to enter the U.S. will undergo public health entry screening at one of the following U.S. airports: Washington-Dulles International Airport (IAD) outside Washington, D.C. Hartsfield-Jackson Atlanta International Airport (ATL) in Atlanta, Georgia George Bush Intercontinental Airport (IAH) in Houston, Texas John F. Kennedy International Airport (JFK) in New York, New York NOTE: Travelers who were in DRC, South Sudan, or Uganda more than 21 days before traveling to the United States will not need to undergo public health entry screening. During entry screening, travelers will: Be escorted to a screening area Complete a brief questionnaire about travel history and symptoms, if any Have their temperature checked Be observed for signs of illness by CDC staff Be enrolled in automated text messages from CDC to remind them to monitor their health for 21 days after they left the affected country. Some travelers will have an additional public health assessment based on their area of travel and activities while in the affected country. CDC will also share travelers' contact information with state or local public health authorities, as needed, for follow-up and support. If you do not have symptoms You will receive information about monitoring your health for 21 days after leaving the affected country. Most travelers without symptoms will continue to their final destination after public health entry screening. If you have symptoms If you have a fever or other symptoms consistent with Ebola, a CDC public health medical officer will conduct an additional evaluation. If Ebola is suspected, you will be transferred to a hospital for medical evaluation, isolation, and care. Steps to take after travel Monitor your health Monitor your health for 21 days after you leave DRC,* South Sudan or Uganda. After you arrive in the U.S., CDC will send you automated text messages reminding you to watch for symptoms and to take action if you get sick. Take your temperature If you were in DRC, take your temperature every day and again if you feel sick. If you were in South Sudan or Uganda, take your temperature if you feel sick. Watch for symptoms Watch for possible symptoms of Ebola such as fever (100.4°F/38°C or higher) or feeling feverish, headache or body aches, rash, weakness or tiredness, sore throat, diarrhea, vomiting, stomach pain, or unexplained bleeding or bruising (a late stage of illness). *People who have been granted an exception to travel to the United States. Travel During Your Monitoring Period CDC recommends that you remain in the United States and avoid international travel and cruise ship travel during your 21-day monitoring period. If you are in the U.S. and plan to travel domestically or internationally while you are being monitored, notify your health department before you leave. If you have been in DRC and leave the United States before completing the 21-day period, you will not be permitted to return until 21 days have passed since you left DRC. What to do if you get sick If you develop any signs or symptoms that might be Ebola: Separate yourself from others (isolate). Stay in a room by yourself and don't share a bathroom with others. Call your local health department (if you are in the United States) or health officials where you are located for advice about medical care. Tell the health department about your recent travel from a country with risk of Ebola and your symptoms. They can identify the best healthcare facility for you to go to, if needed. Health department staff can also help the facility prepare to provide care for you and take any health precautions needed. Follow all their instructions. Do not travel while sick. Travel only when you have been cleared by a doctor or health official. If you need urgent medical care and you can't reach your health department right away Call a healthcare provider. If you can't reach a healthcare provider, call 911. Tell them your symptoms and that you were recently in a country with risk of Ebola. Calling ahead before going to a healthcare facility helps the facility prepare for your arrival, including contacting the health department and taking any precautions needed.
